Hi. Um, well, I’m here because I keep having these nights where I eat an amount of food that honestly just scares me. Like, I’ll start with some leftovers after I finish grading papers... I teach third grade, and right now they are doing this huge project on the solar system, which is great, but the amount of glitter and glue I end up taking home on my clothes is just insane. I swear I vacuumed my living room three times this week and there are still little silver stars in my carpet. 

But, um, anyway, where was I? Oh, right. After grading, I’ll start eating and I just end up going through so much stuff. Like, a whole box of cereal, sleeves of crackers, half a jar of peanut butter, and even whatever snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is actually a huge problem because I promised the kids fruit snacks for their Friday reward, and if you forget a promise to an eight-year-old, they literally never let you live it down. I have to make a whole separate trip to Target tonight just to restock. But yeah, the eating thing... it's just really getting out of control.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable, put-together teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, "Joan, stop." But my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway. I literally can't stop until I'm so full it actually hurts. 

Which, honestly, is kind of how I feel when I'm trying to get my kids to stop talking during silent reading time. You tell them to stop, and they just keep going! There's this one kid, Leo, who always brings these little plastic dinosaurs to class, and he makes them fight under his desk. I've told him a hundred times they need to stay in his backpack, but it's like he doesn't even hear me. He just keeps making these little roaring noises. It's adorable, but also so distracting for the other kids. I had to email his mom about it last week, and she was so apologetic, but I swear she sounded just as exhausted as I am. But yeah, the eating... it's just like I have zero control over it once it starts.

Um, well... I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. 

So yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. But I don't know if the eating is always because of that. Sometimes it just happens, you know? Even on good days. Like last week when we had the school bake sale. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. Anyway, I don't really know if there's a specific feeling that triggers it. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that.

I teach third grade, and right now they are doing this huge project on the solar system, which is great, but the amount of glitter and glue I end up taking home on my clothes is just insane. I swear I vacuumed my living room three times this week and there are still little silver stars in my carpet. \n\nBut, um, anyway, where was I? Oh, right. After grading, I’ll start eating and I just end up going through so much stuff. Like, a whole box of cereal, sleeves of crackers, half a jar of peanut butter, and even whatever snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is actually a huge problem because I promised the kids fruit snacks for their Friday reward, and if you forget a promise to an eight-year-old, they literally never let you live it down. I have to make a whole separate trip to Target tonight just to restock. But yeah, the eating thing... it's just really getting out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable, put-together teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, \"Joan, stop.\" But my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway. I literally can't stop until I'm so full it actually hurts. \n\nWhich, honestly, is kind of how I feel when I'm trying to get my kids to stop talking during silent reading time. You tell them to stop, and they just keep going! There's this one kid, Leo, who always brings these little plastic dinosaurs to class, and he makes them fight under his desk. I've told him a hundred times they need to stay in his backpack, but it's like he doesn't even hear me. He just keeps making these little roaring noises. It's adorable, but also so distracting for the other kids. I had to email his mom about it last week, and she was so apologetic, but I swear she sounded just as exhausted as I am. But yeah, the eating... it's just like I have zero control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating usually happen when you're feeling stressed or upset about something?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well... I guess I just feel really, really awful about myself. Like, physically I feel sick because I'm so full, but mentally, I just feel this overwhelming guilt. It's so heavy. And honestly, it makes me feel like such a fraud when I go into work the next day. 

Because, you know, part of being a third-grade teacher is teaching them about healthy habits. We actually just finished a unit on the food pyramid—well, I guess it's "MyPlate" now, which is still so hard for me to get used to because I grew up with the pyramid. Remember the pyramid? With the huge section for bread and pasta at the bottom? I used to love drawing that when I was a kid. I had this set of Mr. Sketch scented markers, and I would always use the blue one for the dairy section because it smelled like blueberries, even though milk definitely doesn't smell like blueberries. Unless it's gone bad, I guess, but then it smells terrible. One time, the cafeteria fridge broke over the weekend, and when we came in on Monday, the smell in the hallway was just... unforgettable. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to use this industrial deodorizer that smelled like fake pine needles for a week. 

But yeah, anyway... after I eat like that, I just feel so disgusted with myself. I don't really know how else to describe it. Just really ashamed.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was saying before, it's just... no. No control at all. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, "Joan, you need to stop." But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. 

Which is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing "Let It Go" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. 

But anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.

I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. But I don't know if the eating is always because of that. Sometimes it just happens, you know? Even on good days. Like last week when we had the school bake sale. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. Anyway, I don't really know if there's a specific feeling that triggers it. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes, once you've stopped eating and are feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just feel really, really awful about myself. Like, physically I feel sick because I'm so full, but mentally, I just feel this overwhelming guilt. It's so heavy. And honestly, it makes me feel like such a fraud when I go into work the next day. \n\nBecause, you know, part of being a third-grade teacher is teaching them about healthy habits. We actually just finished a unit on the food pyramid—well, I guess it's \"MyPlate\" now, which is still so hard for me to get used to because I grew up with the pyramid. Remember the pyramid? With the huge section for bread and pasta at the bottom? I used to love drawing that when I was a kid. I had this set of Mr. Sketch scented markers, and I would always use the blue one for the dairy section because it smelled like blueberries, even though milk definitely doesn't smell like blueberries. Unless it's gone bad, I guess, but then it smells terrible. One time, the cafeteria fridge broke over the weekend, and when we came in on Monday, the smell in the hallway was just... unforgettable. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to use this industrial deodorizer that smelled like fake pine needles for a week. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... after I eat like that, I just feel so disgusted with myself. I don't really know how else to describe it. Just really ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating at all during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, it's just... no. No control at all. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, \"Joan, you need to stop.\" But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. 

Which is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a "feelings check-in" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says "OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY." She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. 

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.

Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. 

It's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. Like, I’ll start with some leftovers after I finish grading papers... I teach third grade, and right now they are doing this huge project on the solar system, which is great, but the amount of glitter and glue I end up taking home on my clothes is just insane. Which is actually a huge problem because I promised the kids fruit snacks for their Friday reward, and if you forget a promise to an eight-year-old, they literally never let you live it down. I have to make a whole separate trip to Target tonight just to restock. But yeah, the eating thing... it's just really getting out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable, put-together teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, \"Joan, stop.\" But my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway. I literally can't stop until I'm so full it actually hurts. \n\nWhich, honestly, is kind of how I feel when I'm trying to get my kids to stop talking during silent reading time. You tell them to stop, and they just keep going! There's this one kid, Leo, who always brings these little plastic dinosaurs to class, and he makes them fight under his desk. I've told him a hundred times they need to stay in his backpack, but it's like he doesn't even hear me. He just keeps making these little roaring noises. It's adorable, but also so distracting for the other kids. I had to email his mom about it last week, and she was so apologetic, but I swear she sounded just as exhausted as I am. But yeah, the eating... it's just like I have zero control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating usually happen when you're feeling stressed or upset about something?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. But I don't know if the eating is always because of that. Sometimes it just happens, you know? Even on good days. Like last week when we had the school bake sale. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. Anyway, I don't really know if there's a specific feeling that triggers it. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes, once you've stopped eating and are feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just feel really, really awful about myself. Like, physically I feel sick because I'm so full, but mentally, I just feel this overwhelming guilt. It's so heavy. And honestly, it makes me feel like such a fraud when I go into work the next day. \n\nBecause, you know, part of being a third-grade teacher is teaching them about healthy habits. We actually just finished a unit on the food pyramid—well, I guess it's \"MyPlate\" now, which is still so hard for me to get used to because I grew up with the pyramid. Remember the pyramid? With the huge section for bread and pasta at the bottom? I used to love drawing that when I was a kid. I had this set of Mr. Sketch scented markers, and I would always use the blue one for the dairy section because it smelled like blueberries, even though milk definitely doesn't smell like blueberries. Unless it's gone bad, I guess, but then it smells terrible. One time, the cafeteria fridge broke over the weekend, and when we came in on Monday, the smell in the hallway was just... unforgettable. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to use this industrial deodorizer that smelled like fake pine needles for a week. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... after I eat like that, I just feel so disgusted with myself. I don't really know how else to describe it. Just really ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating at all during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, it's just... no. No control at all. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, \"Joan, you need to stop.\" But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a \"feelings check-in\" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. My grandmother used to swear by shortening for her pie crusts, which did make them super flaky, but for cookies, you really need real butter. I usually buy that Irish butter in the gold foil? But it's gotten so expensive. I saw it at the store the other day and it was almost eight dollars. Eight dollars for a block of butter! It's just ridiculous how much groceries cost right now. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, if I always feel guilty. Yeah, I'm just not sure if it's every single time. Sometimes I'm just too tired to feel much of anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Um, wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like, about the emotions? I think I just... yeah, I really haven't noticed if it's sadness or anxiety or whatever. It's just hard to tell. 

Which is so frustrating because, like I said, we're doing all this feelings stuff at school. The kids actually love it, though. Well, most of them. Maya—the one who sang "Let It Go"—she decided her feeling color is "sparkle," which isn't even on the chart. But what are you going to do? You have to pick your battles with eight-year-olds. Last week, it was so windy my scarf blew right off my neck and got stuck in the chain-link fence. It was my favorite scarf, too—this really soft cashmere one my sister got me for Christmas. She always gets the best gifts. She works in marketing, so she always knows what's trendy before anyone else does. She actually tried to get me to move to the city with her after grad school, but I just couldn't imagine teaching in a huge district like that.

You tell them to stop, and they just keep going! There's this one kid, Leo, who always brings these little plastic dinosaurs to class, and he makes them fight under his desk. I've told him a hundred times they need to stay in his backpack, but it's like he doesn't even hear me. He just keeps making these little roaring noises. It's adorable, but also so distracting for the other kids. I had to email his mom about it last week, and she was so apologetic, but I swear she sounded just as exhausted as I am. But yeah, the eating... it's just like I have zero control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating usually happen when you're feeling stressed or upset about something?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. But I don't know if the eating is always because of that. Sometimes it just happens, you know? Even on good days. Like last week when we had the school bake sale. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. Anyway, I don't really know if there's a specific feeling that triggers it. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes, once you've stopped eating and are feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just feel really, really awful about myself. Like, physically I feel sick because I'm so full, but mentally, I just feel this overwhelming guilt. It's so heavy. And honestly, it makes me feel like such a fraud when I go into work the next day. \n\nBecause, you know, part of being a third-grade teacher is teaching them about healthy habits. We actually just finished a unit on the food pyramid—well, I guess it's \"MyPlate\" now, which is still so hard for me to get used to because I grew up with the pyramid. Remember the pyramid? With the huge section for bread and pasta at the bottom? I used to love drawing that when I was a kid. I had this set of Mr. Sketch scented markers, and I would always use the blue one for the dairy section because it smelled like blueberries, even though milk definitely doesn't smell like blueberries. Unless it's gone bad, I guess, but then it smells terrible. One time, the cafeteria fridge broke over the weekend, and when we came in on Monday, the smell in the hallway was just... unforgettable. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to use this industrial deodorizer that smelled like fake pine needles for a week. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... after I eat like that, I just feel so disgusted with myself. I don't really know how else to describe it. Just really ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating at all during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, it's just... no. No control at all. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, \"Joan, you need to stop.\" But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a \"feelings check-in\" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. My grandmother used to swear by shortening for her pie crusts, which did make them super flaky, but for cookies, you really need real butter. I usually buy that Irish butter in the gold foil? But it's gotten so expensive. I saw it at the store the other day and it was almost eight dollars. Eight dollars for a block of butter! It's just ridiculous how much groceries cost right now. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, if I always feel guilty. Yeah, I'm just not sure if it's every single time. Sometimes I'm just too tired to feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotion, such as anxiety or sadness?\n\nPatient: Um, wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like, about the emotions? I think I just... yeah, I really haven't noticed if it's sadness or anxiety or whatever. It's just hard to tell. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because, like I said, we're doing all this feelings stuff at school. The kids actually love it, though. Well, most of them. Maya—the one who sang \"Let It Go\"—she decided her feeling color is \"sparkle,\" which isn't even on the chart. But what are you going to do? You have to pick your battles with eight-year-olds. If you fight them on everything, you just end up exhausted by recess. And recess duty is the worst anyway. You just stand out there in the freezing cold while they run around like maniacs. Last week, it was so windy my scarf blew right off my neck and got stuck in the chain-link fence. It was my favorite scarf, too—this really soft cashmere one my sister got me for Christmas. She always gets the best gifts. She works in marketing, so she always knows what's trendy before anyone else does. She actually tried to get me to move to the city with her after grad school, but I just couldn't imagine teaching in a huge district like that. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I just really haven't noticed a specific emotion that triggers it. I wish I had a better answer for you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. Maya—the one who sang \"Let It Go\"—she decided her feeling color is \"sparkle,\" which isn't even on the chart. But what are you going to do? You have to pick your battles with eight-year-olds. Last week, it was so windy my scarf blew right off my neck and got stuck in the chain-link fence. It was my favorite scarf, too—this really soft cashmere one my sister got me for Christmas. She always gets the best gifts. She works in marketing, so she always knows what's trendy before anyone else does. She actually tried to get me to move to the city with her after grad school, but I just couldn't imagine teaching in a huge district like that. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, it's definitely not a choice. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Just standing there saying, "Joan, stop," but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. I just can't stop it once it starts. 

It's actually kind of like how the kids are when someone brings in cupcakes for a birthday. It's just complete chaos. Last week it was Leo's birthday, and his mom brought in these massive cupcakes with that really thick, stiff frosting. You know the kind that stains your teeth and hands blue for two days? It was a disaster. I had to send three kids to the nurse because they got frosting in their hair, and one kid somehow got it on his shoe and tracked it all over the reading rug. That rug was so expensive, too. I bought it myself at Target during their back-to-school sale. They always have the cutest stuff in that dollar spot section at the front, but you have to get there in July or all the good bins and borders are gone. I spent like forty dollars just on these little motivational posters with sloths on them. Sloths are huge this year for some reason. Last year it was llamas. I don't know who decides these trends, but I swear half my paycheck goes to keeping my classroom looking cute. 

But, um, yeah. It's definitely a loss of control. I'm not choosing to keep eating. It just happens and I can't stop it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I’m here because I keep having these nights where I eat an amount of food that honestly just scares me. Like, I’ll start with some leftovers after I finish grading papers... I teach third grade, and right now they are doing this huge project on the solar system, which is great, but the amount of glitter and glue I end up taking home on my clothes is just insane. I swear I vacuumed my living room three times this week and there are still little silver stars in my carpet. \n\nBut, um, anyway, where was I? Oh, right. After grading, I’ll start eating and I just end up going through so much stuff. Like, a whole box of cereal, sleeves of crackers, half a jar of peanut butter, and even whatever snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is actually a huge problem because I promised the kids fruit snacks for their Friday reward, and if you forget a promise to an eight-year-old, they literally never let you live it down. I have to make a whole separate trip to Target tonight just to restock. But yeah, the eating thing... it's just really getting out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable, put-together teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, \"Joan, stop.\" But my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway. I literally can't stop until I'm so full it actually hurts. \n\nWhich, honestly, is kind of how I feel when I'm trying to get my kids to stop talking during silent reading time. You tell them to stop, and they just keep going! There's this one kid, Leo, who always brings these little plastic dinosaurs to class, and he makes them fight under his desk. I've told him a hundred times they need to stay in his backpack, but it's like he doesn't even hear me. He just keeps making these little roaring noises. It's adorable, but also so distracting for the other kids. I had to email his mom about it last week, and she was so apologetic, but I swear she sounded just as exhausted as I am. But yeah, the eating... it's just like I have zero control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating usually happen when you're feeling stressed or upset about something?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. But I don't know if the eating is always because of that. Sometimes it just happens, you know? Even on good days. Like last week when we had the school bake sale. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. Anyway, I don't really know if there's a specific feeling that triggers it. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes, once you've stopped eating and are feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just feel really, really awful about myself. Like, physically I feel sick because I'm so full, but mentally, I just feel this overwhelming guilt. It's so heavy. And honestly, it makes me feel like such a fraud when I go into work the next day. \n\nBecause, you know, part of being a third-grade teacher is teaching them about healthy habits. We actually just finished a unit on the food pyramid—well, I guess it's \"MyPlate\" now, which is still so hard for me to get used to because I grew up with the pyramid. Remember the pyramid? With the huge section for bread and pasta at the bottom? I used to love drawing that when I was a kid. I had this set of Mr. Sketch scented markers, and I would always use the blue one for the dairy section because it smelled like blueberries, even though milk definitely doesn't smell like blueberries. Unless it's gone bad, I guess, but then it smells terrible. One time, the cafeteria fridge broke over the weekend, and when we came in on Monday, the smell in the hallway was just... unforgettable. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to use this industrial deodorizer that smelled like fake pine needles for a week. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... after I eat like that, I just feel so disgusted with myself. I don't really know how else to describe it. Just really ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating at all during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, it's just... no. No control at all. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, \"Joan, you need to stop.\" But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a \"feelings check-in\" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. My grandmother used to swear by shortening for her pie crusts, which did make them super flaky, but for cookies, you really need real butter. I usually buy that Irish butter in the gold foil? But it's gotten so expensive. I saw it at the store the other day and it was almost eight dollars. Eight dollars for a block of butter! It's just ridiculous how much groceries cost right now. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, if I always feel guilty. Yeah, I'm just not sure if it's every single time. Um, I don't really know if it's stress or being overwhelmed, either. Like I said before, I just haven't really noticed a specific feeling that starts it all. I wish I could give you a better answer, but it's just really hard to pinpoint. 

It's kind of like trying to figure out why the copy machine at school jams. It just happens, and you never know what caused it. And our copy machine is ancient. I'm pretty sure it's older than some of the newer teachers. Last week, it jammed right before I needed to print out the math worksheets for our fractions unit. Fractions are always so hard for the kids to grasp. I tried using those little plastic pie pieces to show them, but half the pieces are missing from the math kits. I think the kids from last year might have taken them. Kids love taking little things like that. One time I found a kid with a pocket full of those counting bears. The red ones, specifically. I don't know why he only wanted the red ones. Maybe it was his favorite color? My favorite color is green, like a really deep emerald green. My mom had this emerald green velvet dress she used to wear to holiday parties when I was little, and I always thought she looked like a movie star in it. I wonder if she still has it. I should ask her. She's been wanting me to come over and help her clean out the attic anyway. 

But, um... yeah, no. I really haven't noticed if it's stress or feeling overwhelmed that triggers the eating. I just don't know.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I’m here because I keep having these nights where I eat an amount of food that honestly just scares me. Like, I’ll start with some leftovers after I finish grading papers... I teach third grade, and right now they are doing this huge project on the solar system, which is great, but the amount of glitter and glue I end up taking home on my clothes is just insane. I swear I vacuumed my living room three times this week and there are still little silver stars in my carpet. \n\nBut, um, anyway, where was I? Oh, right. After grading, I’ll start eating and I just end up going through so much stuff. Like, a whole box of cereal, sleeves of crackers, half a jar of peanut butter, and even whatever snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is actually a huge problem because I promised the kids fruit snacks for their Friday reward, and if you forget a promise to an eight-year-old, they literally never let you live it down. I have to make a whole separate trip to Target tonight just to restock. But yeah, the eating thing... it's just really getting out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable, put-together teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, \"Joan, stop.\" But my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway. I literally can't stop until I'm so full it actually hurts. \n\nWhich, honestly, is kind of how I feel when I'm trying to get my kids to stop talking during silent reading time. You tell them to stop, and they just keep going! There's this one kid, Leo, who always brings these little plastic dinosaurs to class, and he makes them fight under his desk. I've told him a hundred times they need to stay in his backpack, but it's like he doesn't even hear me. He just keeps making these little roaring noises. It's adorable, but also so distracting for the other kids. I had to email his mom about it last week, and she was so apologetic, but I swear she sounded just as exhausted as I am. But yeah, the eating... it's just like I have zero control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating usually happen when you're feeling stressed or upset about something?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. But I don't know if the eating is always because of that. Sometimes it just happens, you know? Even on good days. Like last week when we had the school bake sale. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. Anyway, I don't really know if there's a specific feeling that triggers it. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes, once you've stopped eating and are feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just feel really, really awful about myself. Like, physically I feel sick because I'm so full, but mentally, I just feel this overwhelming guilt. It's so heavy. And honestly, it makes me feel like such a fraud when I go into work the next day. \n\nBecause, you know, part of being a third-grade teacher is teaching them about healthy habits. We actually just finished a unit on the food pyramid—well, I guess it's \"MyPlate\" now, which is still so hard for me to get used to because I grew up with the pyramid. Remember the pyramid? With the huge section for bread and pasta at the bottom? I used to love drawing that when I was a kid. I had this set of Mr. Sketch scented markers, and I would always use the blue one for the dairy section because it smelled like blueberries, even though milk definitely doesn't smell like blueberries. Unless it's gone bad, I guess, but then it smells terrible. One time, the cafeteria fridge broke over the weekend, and when we came in on Monday, the smell in the hallway was just... unforgettable. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to use this industrial deodorizer that smelled like fake pine needles for a week. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... after I eat like that, I just feel so disgusted with myself. I don't really know how else to describe it. Just really ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating at all during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, it's just... no. No control at all. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, \"Joan, you need to stop.\" But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a \"feelings check-in\" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. My grandmother used to swear by shortening for her pie crusts, which did make them super flaky, but for cookies, you really need real butter. I usually buy that Irish butter in the gold foil? But it's gotten so expensive. I saw it at the store the other day and it was almost eight dollars. Eight dollars for a block of butter! It's just ridiculous how much groceries cost right now. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, if I always feel guilty. Yeah, I'm just not sure if it's every single time. Sometimes I'm just too tired to feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotion, such as anxiety or sadness?\n\nPatient: Um, wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like, about the emotions? I think I just... yeah, I really haven't noticed if it's sadness or anxiety or whatever. It's just hard to tell. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because, like I said, we're doing all this feelings stuff at school. The kids actually love it, though. Well, most of them. Maya—the one who sang \"Let It Go\"—she decided her feeling color is \"sparkle,\" which isn't even on the chart. But what are you going to do? You have to pick your battles with eight-year-olds. If you fight them on everything, you just end up exhausted by recess. And recess duty is the worst anyway. You just stand out there in the freezing cold while they run around like maniacs. Last week, it was so windy my scarf blew right off my neck and got stuck in the chain-link fence. It was my favorite scarf, too—this really soft cashmere one my sister got me for Christmas. She always gets the best gifts. She works in marketing, so she always knows what's trendy before anyone else does. She actually tried to get me to move to the city with her after grad school, but I just couldn't imagine teaching in a huge district like that. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I just really haven't noticed a specific emotion that triggers it. I wish I had a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control of your eating during these episodes, or is it more that you're making choices to continue eating despite feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it's definitely not a choice. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? It was a disaster. I had to send three kids to the nurse because they got frosting in their hair, and one kid somehow got it on his shoe and tracked it all over the reading rug. That rug was so expensive, too. I bought it myself at Target during their back-to-school sale. They always have the cutest stuff in that dollar spot section at the front, but you have to get there in July or all the good bins and borders are gone. I don't know who decides these trends, but I swear half my paycheck goes to keeping my classroom looking cute. \n\nBut, um, yeah. It's definitely a loss of control. I'm not choosing to keep eating. It just happens and I can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed, rather than another emotion?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's stress or being overwhelmed, either. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, wait, I think we just talked about this, right? The control thing? Yeah, I'm definitely not trying to eat that much on purpose, and it's not about paying attention. Like I said before, it's just... once I start, it's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside. Just standing there going, "Joan, stop," but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm so full it hurts. I just can't stop.

Indoor recess is a nightmare. They just play Heads Up Seven Up for twenty minutes, but someone always ends up crying because they think someone peeked at their shoes. Kids are so obsessed with shoes now. One of my fourth graders has these light-up sneakers that he charges with a USB cord. Can you imagine? Having to plug your shoes in at night? When I was their age, we just had those jelly sandals that gave you blisters if you wore them without socks. I had a pink pair that I wore to Disney World when I was seven, and my dad had to carry me through Epcot because my feet hurt so bad. Epcot is really boring when you're seven, anyway. It's just a bunch of fake countries and that big golf ball thing. 

But yeah, anyway, it's definitely not intentional. It's just that loss of control, like I'm watching myself do it and I can't make it stop.
