They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a \"feelings check-in\" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. My grandmother used to swear by shortening for her pie crusts, which did make them super flaky, but for cookies, you really need real butter. I usually buy that Irish butter in the gold foil? It's just ridiculous how much groceries cost right now. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, if I always feel guilty. Yeah, I'm just not sure if it's every single time. It's just hard to tell. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because, like I said, we're doing all this feelings stuff at school. The kids actually love it, though. Well, most of them. Maya—the one who sang \"Let It Go\"—she decided her feeling color is \"sparkle,\" which isn't even on the chart. But what are you going to do? You have to pick your battles with eight-year-olds. If you fight them on everything, you just end up exhausted by recess. And recess duty is the worst anyway. You just stand out there in the freezing cold while they run around like maniacs. Last week, it was so windy my scarf blew right off my neck and got stuck in the chain-link fence. It was my favorite scarf, too—this really soft cashmere one my sister got me for Christmas. She always gets the best gifts. She works in marketing, so she always knows what's trendy before anyone else does. She actually tried to get me to move to the city with her after grad school, but I just couldn't imagine teaching in a huge district like that. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I just really haven't noticed a specific emotion that triggers it. I wish I had a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control of your eating during these episodes, or is it more that you're making choices to continue eating despite feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it's definitely not a choice. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Just standing there saying, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. I just can't stop it once it starts. \n\nIt's actually kind of like how the kids are when someone brings in cupcakes for a birthday. It's just complete chaos. It was a disaster. I had to send three kids to the nurse because they got frosting in their hair, and one kid somehow got it on his shoe and tracked it all over the reading rug. That rug was so expensive, too. I bought it myself at Target during their back-to-school sale. They always have the cutest stuff in that dollar spot section at the front, but you have to get there in July or all the good bins and borders are gone. I spent like forty dollars just on these little motivational posters with sloths on them. Sloths are huge this year for some reason. Last year it was llamas. I don't know who decides these trends, but I swear half my paycheck goes to keeping my classroom looking cute. \n\nBut, um, yeah. It's definitely a loss of control. I'm not choosing to keep eating. It just happens and I can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed, rather than another emotion?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's stress or being overwhelmed, either. Like I said before, I just haven't really noticed a specific feeling that starts it all. I wish I could give you a better answer, but it's just really hard to pinpoint. \n\nIt's kind of like trying to figure out why the copy machine at school jams. It just happens, and you never know what caused it. And our copy machine is ancient. I'm pretty sure it's older than some of the newer teachers. Last week, it jammed right before I needed to print out the math worksheets for our fractions unit. Fractions are always so hard for the kids to grasp. I tried using those little plastic pie pieces to show them, but half the pieces are missing from the math kits. I think the kids from last year might have taken them. Kids love taking little things like that. One time I found a kid with a pocket full of those counting bears. The red ones, specifically. I don't know why he only wanted the red ones. Maybe it was his favorite color? My favorite color is green, like a really deep emerald green. My mom had this emerald green velvet dress she used to wear to holiday parties when I was little, and I always thought she looked like a movie star in it. I wonder if she still has it. I should ask her. She's been wanting me to come over and help her clean out the attic anyway. \n\nBut, um... yeah, no. I really haven't noticed if it's stress or feeling overwhelmed that triggers the eating. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you're not paying attention to how full you're getting, or is it more that you're intentionally trying to eat as much as you can?\n\nPatient: Um, wait, I think we just talked about this, right? The control thing? Yeah, I'm definitely not trying to eat that much on purpose, and it's not about paying attention. Like I said before, it's just... once I start, it's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside. I just can't stop. \n\nWhich is exactly what I tell my kids when they're running in the halls—\"You have to stop your bodies!\" They just don't listen sometimes. Especially on rainy days when we have indoor recess. Indoor recess is a nightmare. They just play Heads Up Seven Up for twenty minutes, but someone always ends up crying because they think someone peeked at their shoes. Kids are so obsessed with shoes now. One of my fourth graders has these light-up sneakers that he charges with a USB cord. Can you imagine? Having to plug your shoes in at night? When I was their age, we just had those jelly sandals that gave you blisters if you wore them without socks. I had a pink pair that I wore to Disney World when I was seven, and my dad had to carry me through Epcot because my feet hurt so bad. Epcot is really boring when you're seven, anyway. It's just a bunch of fake countries and that big golf ball thing. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's definitely not intentional. It's just that loss of control, like I'm watching myself do it and I can't make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you're in a state where you're not thinking clearly about your actions?\n\nPatient: Um, I feel like we're kind of going in circles here? I know I already said this a couple of times, but it's not really about thinking clearly or not thinking clearly. It's just... I *am* thinking, but I can't do anything about it. It's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just standing there watching from the outside, literally saying, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep going. They just keep opening wrappers and putting food in my mouth until I'm so full it's actually painful. I just can't stop it.\n\nWhich is so frustrating, because normally I'm the one in charge, you know? Like during the fire drills at school. I have to be the one directing everyone, making sure all twenty-two kids are lined up and quiet. We had a fire drill last month right in the middle of our spelling test, which was a total disaster because half the kids left their pencils on their desks and then couldn't find them when we came back inside. I always buy those big boxes of Ticonderoga pencils—they're the only ones that don't break every five seconds in the sharpener. The cheap ones always snap, and then you have a line of kids waiting at the sharpener, which is right next to the guinea pig cage. We have a class guinea pig named Barnaby. He squeaks every time someone sharpens a pencil because he thinks the noise means he's getting a carrot. Guinea pigs are so funny like that. My sister had one when we were growing up, but it escaped and lived under our sofa for three days before my dad finally caught it with a fishing net. \n\nBut, um, yeah. Like I said, I *am* thinking clearly when it happens, I just... I can't make myself stop eating. It's just completely out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating affect your daily life or cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, it definitely causes a lot of distress. It really messes with everything. Like, my evenings and my weekends, they just feel completely planned around trying to undo whatever happened with the food. So I've been getting really behind on things. I'm way slower with my grading, which is stressful because report cards are coming up. And I'm just less present with my friends, too. I'm constantly worried about making sure I still seem totally put-together at work, you know? \n\nWhich is exhausting, honestly. Being put-together at school is basically a full-time job in itself. You have to look like you know what you're doing at all times, even when the projector stops working in the middle of a lesson. Our projector is always breaking. The bulb went out last Tuesday, and the tech guy, Mr. Henderson, took three days to come fix it. He's always so slow. I think he spends most of his time in his office playing solitaire on his computer. I saw him doing it once when I went down there to ask for more whiteboard markers. We go through whiteboard markers so fast. The kids love to push too hard on the tips and ruin them. Especially the blue ones. Why is it always the blue ones? Blue is such a calming color, though. I painted my bedroom a really soft, pale blue last summer. It took me three coats to get it right because the old paint underneath was this terrible mustard yellow. Who paints a bedroom mustard yellow? It looked like a giant hot dog stand. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. It affects my life a lot. I'm just always worried about hiding it and trying to fix it, and it makes it hard to keep up with work and my friends.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's pretty frequent. For the past few months, there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I eat way, way more than I ever meant to. And then, of course, the next day I try to make up for it by just not eating at school, or pushing myself through a really long walk or extra yoga when I get home. \n\nYoga is supposed to be so relaxing, right? But I go to this studio downtown, and the instructor, she always plays this really aggressive, thumping electronic music during the vinyasa flow. It's so weird. You're trying to hold a warrior pose and breathe, and it sounds like you're at a nightclub in Berlin. I went to Berlin once during college for a study abroad trip. We took a train there from Paris, and I lost my favorite scarf on that train. It was this beautiful chunky knit infinity scarf that my grandmother made me. She was always knitting things. She knitted me a sweater once that had one sleeve longer than the other, but I wore it anyway because she was so proud of it. She passed away a few years ago, actually. We had to clean out her house, and you wouldn't believe how much yarn one person can hoard. It was like a craft store exploded in her guest bedroom. We ended up donating most of it to a local nursing home. I hope they made some nice blankets or something out of it. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so it's at least once a week, usually, that I have one of those nights, and then I have to do the fasting or the extra exercise the next day to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah. I mean, it's terrible. I feel so guilty, and I'm just so hard on myself afterwards. Because it all comes back to how I look, you know? Like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't matter how well my students are doing or how prepared my lesson plans are. If my clothes are tight, I just feel like a total failure. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the kids do not care what I look like. They just care if I brought stickers. I have this huge binder of scratch-and-sniff stickers. The grape ones are the most popular, obviously. But I bought this one pack online that was supposed to smell like pizza, and it was the most disgusting thing I've ever smelled in my life. It smelled like old garlic and sweaty gym socks. I gave one to a kid, Tommy, and he stuck it on his forehead and then cried because the smell wouldn't wash off. His mom had to email me about it. Parents send the wildest emails sometimes. I got one last week from a mom asking if I could make sure her daughter only drinks room-temperature water from the fountain because cold water \"shocks her aura.\" Like, how am I supposed to control the temperature of the school water fountain? The fountain in our hallway barely even works anyway. You have to press the button at the exact right angle or it just makes this loud groaning noise and spits out a tiny trickle. It sounds like a dying whale. I saw a documentary about whales once on Netflix. They have these incredibly complex songs that can travel for miles underwater. It's fascinating how they communicate. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. The guilt is really bad. I just judge myself so harshly if my body changes even a little bit, and it completely ruins my confidence, even when I know I'm doing a good job at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating because you're hungry or because you just can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not because I'm hungry. It's... it's like a total loss of control. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. It's like I'm on autopilot. \n\nAutopilot is such a weird concept, isn't it? Like, when you're driving home from work and you suddenly pull into your driveway and realize you don't remember the last ten minutes of the drive. That happens to me all the time on Elm Street. Elm Street has all those huge, old oak trees that hang over the road, and in the fall, the leaves get so thick on the ground it's like driving on a carpet. The city is terrible at clearing them. Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. That's why I always keep a space heater under my desk at school. The custodian, Mr. Davies, told me it's a fire hazard, but I just hide it behind my filing cabinet when he comes in to empty the trash. He's always complaining about something, usually the amount of glitter we use in art projects. Glitter really is the herpes of the craft world, it just never goes away. I found a piece of purple glitter on my cheek just yesterday, and we haven't done a glitter project since Valentine's Day. Valentine's Day is exhausting in an elementary school. The amount of sugar those kids consume...\n\nBut, um, yeah, anyway. I'm definitely not hungry when it happens. I just can't stop my hands from grabbing the food once I've started, even when my brain is telling me to stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's... it's like a total loss of control. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Like, when you're driving home from work and you suddenly pull into your driveway and realize you don't remember the last ten minutes of the drive. That happens to me all the time on Elm Street. Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. Um, yeah, like I was saying, it's... it's really not a choice. It feels totally out of my hands. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Like I'm standing there saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. 

It's actually a lot like how I feel when I'm trying to organize the supply closet at work. You go in there just needing one pack of dry erase markers, and suddenly you're knee-deep in construction paper and trying to sort out three years' worth of dried-up glue sticks. I don't know why schools buy those purple glue sticks anyway. They say they dry clear, but they always leave this weird crusty residue on the paper. I had a student last year, little Kevin, who used to try to eat the glue sticks. I had to watch him like a hawk during arts and crafts. He also used to chew on his pencils until the metal part holding the eraser was completely flattened. I worry about kids like that, you know? Like, what is going on at home that makes you want to eat glue? I brought it up to the school counselor, Mrs. Higgins, but she's always so busy. She has this tiny office that smells strongly of lavender, which I think is supposed to be calming, but it just gives me a headache. I'm really sensitive to smells. I can't even walk down the detergent aisle at the grocery store without getting a migraine. 

But, um, yeah, so I'm definitely not choosing to keep eating. It just... it just happens, and I can't make it stop.

It's just a bunch of fake countries and that big golf ball thing. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's definitely not intentional. I know I already said this a couple of times, but it's not really about thinking clearly or not thinking clearly. It's just... I *am* thinking, but I can't do anything about it. It's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just standing there watching from the outside, literally saying, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep going. They just keep opening wrappers and putting food in my mouth until I'm so full it's actually painful. I just can't stop it.\n\nWhich is so frustrating, because normally I'm the one in charge, you know? Like during the fire drills at school. I have to be the one directing everyone, making sure all twenty-two kids are lined up and quiet. We had a fire drill last month right in the middle of our spelling test, which was a total disaster because half the kids left their pencils on their desks and then couldn't find them when we came back inside. I always buy those big boxes of Ticonderoga pencils—they're the only ones that don't break every five seconds in the sharpener. The cheap ones always snap, and then you have a line of kids waiting at the sharpener, which is right next to the guinea pig cage. We have a class guinea pig named Barnaby. He squeaks every time someone sharpens a pencil because he thinks the noise means he's getting a carrot. Guinea pigs are so funny like that. My sister had one when we were growing up, but it escaped and lived under our sofa for three days before my dad finally caught it with a fishing net. \n\nBut, um, yeah. Like I said, I *am* thinking clearly when it happens, I just... I can't make myself stop eating. It's just completely out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating affect your daily life or cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, it definitely causes a lot of distress. It really messes with everything. Like, my evenings and my weekends, they just feel completely planned around trying to undo whatever happened with the food. So I've been getting really behind on things. I'm way slower with my grading, which is stressful because report cards are coming up. And I'm just less present with my friends, too. I'm constantly worried about making sure I still seem totally put-together at work, you know? \n\nWhich is exhausting, honestly. Being put-together at school is basically a full-time job in itself. You have to look like you know what you're doing at all times, even when the projector stops working in the middle of a lesson. Our projector is always breaking. The bulb went out last Tuesday, and the tech guy, Mr. Henderson, took three days to come fix it. He's always so slow. I think he spends most of his time in his office playing solitaire on his computer. I saw him doing it once when I went down there to ask for more whiteboard markers. We go through whiteboard markers so fast. The kids love to push too hard on the tips and ruin them. Especially the blue ones. Why is it always the blue ones? Blue is such a calming color, though. I painted my bedroom a really soft, pale blue last summer. It took me three coats to get it right because the old paint underneath was this terrible mustard yellow. Who paints a bedroom mustard yellow? It looked like a giant hot dog stand. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. It affects my life a lot. I'm just always worried about hiding it and trying to fix it, and it makes it hard to keep up with work and my friends.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's pretty frequent. We took a train there from Paris, and I lost my favorite scarf on that train. It was this beautiful chunky knit infinity scarf that my grandmother made me. She was always knitting things. She knitted me a sweater once that had one sleeve longer than the other, but I wore it anyway because she was so proud of it. She passed away a few years ago, actually. We had to clean out her house, and you wouldn't believe how much yarn one person can hoard. It was like a craft store exploded in her guest bedroom. We ended up donating most of it to a local nursing home. I hope they made some nice blankets or something out of it. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so it's at least once a week, usually, that I have one of those nights, and then I have to do the fasting or the extra exercise the next day to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah. I mean, it's terrible. I feel so guilty, and I'm just so hard on myself afterwards. Because it all comes back to how I look, you know? Like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. I got one last week from a mom asking if I could make sure her daughter only drinks room-temperature water from the fountain because cold water \"shocks her aura.\" Like, how am I supposed to control the temperature of the school water fountain? The fountain in our hallway barely even works anyway. You have to press the button at the exact right angle or it just makes this loud groaning noise and spits out a tiny trickle. It sounds like a dying whale. I saw a documentary about whales once on Netflix. They have these incredibly complex songs that can travel for miles underwater. It's fascinating how they communicate. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. The guilt is really bad. I just judge myself so harshly if my body changes even a little bit, and it completely ruins my confidence, even when I know I'm doing a good job at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating because you're hungry or because you just can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not because I'm hungry. Saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. It's like I'm on autopilot. \n\nAutopilot is such a weird concept, isn't it? Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. That's why I always keep a space heater under my desk at school. The custodian, Mr. Davies, told me it's a fire hazard, but I just hide it behind my filing cabinet when he comes in to empty the trash. He's always complaining about something, usually the amount of glitter we use in art projects. Glitter really is the herpes of the craft world, it just never goes away. I found a piece of purple glitter on my cheek just yesterday, and we haven't done a glitter project since Valentine's Day. Valentine's Day is exhausting in an elementary school. The amount of sugar those kids consume...\n\nBut, um, yeah, anyway. I'm definitely not hungry when it happens. I just can't stop my hands from grabbing the food once I've started, even when my brain is telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're deliberately choosing to continue eating during these episodes, or does it feel like you're unable to stop?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, like I was saying, it's... it's really not a choice. He also used to chew on his pencils until the metal part holding the eraser was completely flattened. I worry about kids like that, you know? But I go to this studio downtown, and the instructor, she always plays this really aggressive, thumping electronic music during the vinyasa flow. It's so weird. You're trying to hold a warrior pose and breathe, and it sounds like you're at a nightclub in Berlin. I went to Berlin once during college for a study abroad trip. We took a train there from Paris, and I lost my favorite scarf on that train. It was this beautiful chunky knit infinity scarf that my grandmother made me. She was always knitting things. She knitted me a sweater once that had one sleeve longer than the other, but I wore it anyway because she was so proud of it. She passed away a few years ago, actually. We had to clean out her house, and you wouldn't believe how much yarn one person can hoard. It was like a craft store exploded in her guest bedroom. We ended up donating most of it to a local nursing home. I hope they made some nice blankets or something out of it. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so it's at least once a week, usually, that I have one of those nights, and then I have to do the fasting or the extra exercise the next day to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah. I mean, it's terrible. I feel so guilty, and I'm just so hard on myself afterwards. Because it all comes back to how I look, you know? Like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't matter how well my students are doing or how prepared my lesson plans are. If my clothes are tight, I just feel like a total failure. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the kids do not care what I look like. They just care if I brought stickers. I have this huge binder of scratch-and-sniff stickers. The grape ones are the most popular, obviously. But I bought this one pack online that was supposed to smell like pizza, and it was the most disgusting thing I've ever smelled in my life. It smelled like old garlic and sweaty gym socks. I gave one to a kid, Tommy, and he stuck it on his forehead and then cried because the smell wouldn't wash off. His mom had to email me about it. Parents send the wildest emails sometimes. I got one last week from a mom asking if I could make sure her daughter only drinks room-temperature water from the fountain because cold water \"shocks her aura.\" Like, how am I supposed to control the temperature of the school water fountain? The fountain in our hallway barely even works anyway. You have to press the button at the exact right angle or it just makes this loud groaning noise and spits out a tiny trickle. It sounds like a dying whale. I saw a documentary about whales once on Netflix. They have these incredibly complex songs that can travel for miles underwater. It's fascinating how they communicate. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. The guilt is really bad. I just judge myself so harshly if my body changes even a little bit, and it completely ruins my confidence, even when I know I'm doing a good job at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating because you're hungry or because you just can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not because I'm hungry. It's... it's like a total loss of control. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. It's like I'm on autopilot. \n\nAutopilot is such a weird concept, isn't it? Like, when you're driving home from work and you suddenly pull into your driveway and realize you don't remember the last ten minutes of the drive. That happens to me all the time on Elm Street. Elm Street has all those huge, old oak trees that hang over the road, and in the fall, the leaves get so thick on the ground it's like driving on a carpet. The city is terrible at clearing them. Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. That's why I always keep a space heater under my desk at school. The custodian, Mr. Davies, told me it's a fire hazard, but I just hide it behind my filing cabinet when he comes in to empty the trash. He's always complaining about something, usually the amount of glitter we use in art projects. Glitter really is the herpes of the craft world, it just never goes away. I found a piece of purple glitter on my cheek just yesterday, and we haven't done a glitter project since Valentine's Day. Valentine's Day is exhausting in an elementary school. The amount of sugar those kids consume...\n\nBut, um, yeah, anyway. I'm definitely not hungry when it happens. I just can't stop my hands from grabbing the food once I've started, even when my brain is telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're deliberately choosing to continue eating during these episodes, or does it feel like you're unable to stop?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, like I was saying, it's... it's really not a choice. It feels totally out of my hands. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Like I'm standing there saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. \n\nIt's actually a lot like how I feel when I'm trying to organize the supply closet at work. You go in there just needing one pack of dry erase markers, and suddenly you're knee-deep in construction paper and trying to sort out three years' worth of dried-up glue sticks. I don't know why schools buy those purple glue sticks anyway. They say they dry clear, but they always leave this weird crusty residue on the paper. I had a student last year, little Kevin, who used to try to eat the glue sticks. I had to watch him like a hawk during arts and crafts. He also used to chew on his pencils until the metal part holding the eraser was completely flattened. I worry about kids like that, you know? Like, what is going on at home that makes you want to eat glue? I brought it up to the school counselor, Mrs. Higgins, but she's always so busy. She has this tiny office that smells strongly of lavender, which I think is supposed to be calming, but it just gives me a headache. I'm really sensitive to smells. I can't even walk down the detergent aisle at the grocery store without getting a migraine. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so I'm definitely not choosing to keep eating. It just... it just happens, and I can't make it stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Um, I'm not really sure if there's a specific emotion, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that. It just kind of happens. I mean, I guess I'm always a little stressed, but who isn't? Teaching is stressful. 

Especially right now, because we have state testing coming up, and the administration is breathing down our necks about the math scores. They brought in this new curriculum halfway through the year, which is just brilliant, right? Let's change how we do math in February. It's supposed to be more "inquiry-based," but the kids are just confused, and honestly, so am I. I spent three hours on Sunday just trying to figure out how to explain a fraction model that looks like a weird pizza. Speaking of pizza, they changed the cafeteria vendor this year, and the new pizza is basically cardboard with ketchup on it. The kids actually started a petition to bring back the old pizza. A third grader wrote it! It was so cute, spelled entirely phonetically, but the principal was not amused. He's this very stern guy, Mr. Henderson, who always wears these incredibly loud ties. Like, neon geometric patterns. It's very distracting when he's observing your class. I always wonder where he even buys them. Does he have a special catalog for ugly ties? 

But, um, anyway... yeah. I don't really know if it's tied to being sad or anxious or anything. I just haven't really paid attention to that part of it.

I have to make a whole separate trip to Target tonight just to restock. But yeah, the eating thing... it's just really getting out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. You tell them to stop, and they just keep going! There's this one kid, Leo, who always brings these little plastic dinosaurs to class, and he makes them fight under his desk. I've told him a hundred times they need to stay in his backpack, but it's like he doesn't even hear me. He just keeps making these little roaring noises. It's adorable, but also so distracting for the other kids. I had to email his mom about it last week, and she was so apologetic, but I swear she sounded just as exhausted as I am. But yeah, the eating... it's just like I have zero control over it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating usually happen when you're feeling stressed or upset about something?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'm not really sure if it's stress, exactly? I mean, teaching is stressful, obviously. Like, just yesterday, the principal came in for an unannounced observation right in the middle of our math lesson. And of course, that was the exact moment the smartboard decided to just freeze on this weird, distorted image of a fraction pie. I was trying to fix it, and the kids were getting restless, and then Sarah—she's this very sweet girl but she gets nosebleeds all the time—just started bleeding all over her desk. It was chaos. Total chaos. I had to call the nurse, try to get the smartboard working, and keep 22 other eight-year-olds from panicking about the blood. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I was pretty stressed when I got home. But I don't know if the eating is always because of that. Sometimes it just happens, you know? Even on good days. Like last week when we had the school bake sale. I actually baked these amazing lemon bars—my grandmother's recipe, she used to make them for every family reunion back in Ohio. She had this huge lemon tree in her backyard, which is so weird for Ohio, right? But she managed to keep it alive in this little sunroom she built. Anyway, I don't really know if there's a specific feeling that triggers it. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes, once you've stopped eating and are feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just feel really, really awful about myself. Like, physically I feel sick because I'm so full, but mentally, I just feel this overwhelming guilt. It's so heavy. And honestly, it makes me feel like such a fraud when I go into work the next day. \n\nBecause, you know, part of being a third-grade teacher is teaching them about healthy habits. We actually just finished a unit on the food pyramid—well, I guess it's \"MyPlate\" now, which is still so hard for me to get used to because I grew up with the pyramid. Remember the pyramid? With the huge section for bread and pasta at the bottom? I used to love drawing that when I was a kid. I had this set of Mr. Sketch scented markers, and I would always use the blue one for the dairy section because it smelled like blueberries, even though milk definitely doesn't smell like blueberries. Unless it's gone bad, I guess, but then it smells terrible. One time, the cafeteria fridge broke over the weekend, and when we came in on Monday, the smell in the hallway was just... unforgettable. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, had to use this industrial deodorizer that smelled like fake pine needles for a week. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... after I eat like that, I just feel so disgusted with myself. I don't really know how else to describe it. Just really ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating at all during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, it's just... no. No control at all. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, \"Joan, you need to stop.\" But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a \"feelings check-in\" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. My grandmother used to swear by shortening for her pie crusts, which did make them super flaky, but for cookies, you really need real butter. I usually buy that Irish butter in the gold foil? But it's gotten so expensive. I saw it at the store the other day and it was almost eight dollars. Eight dollars for a block of butter! It's just ridiculous how much groceries cost right now. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, if I always feel guilty. Yeah, I'm just not sure if it's every single time. Sometimes I'm just too tired to feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotion, such as anxiety or sadness?\n\nPatient: Um, wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like, about the emotions? I think I just... yeah, I really haven't noticed if it's sadness or anxiety or whatever. It's just hard to tell. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because, like I said, we're doing all this feelings stuff at school. The kids actually love it, though. Well, most of them. Maya—the one who sang \"Let It Go\"—she decided her feeling color is \"sparkle,\" which isn't even on the chart. But what are you going to do? You have to pick your battles with eight-year-olds. If you fight them on everything, you just end up exhausted by recess. And recess duty is the worst anyway. You just stand out there in the freezing cold while they run around like maniacs. Last week, it was so windy my scarf blew right off my neck and got stuck in the chain-link fence. It was my favorite scarf, too—this really soft cashmere one my sister got me for Christmas. She always gets the best gifts. She works in marketing, so she always knows what's trendy before anyone else does. She actually tried to get me to move to the city with her after grad school, but I just couldn't imagine teaching in a huge district like that. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I just really haven't noticed a specific emotion that triggers it. I wish I had a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control of your eating during these episodes, or is it more that you're making choices to continue eating despite feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it's definitely not a choice. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Just standing there saying, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. I just can't stop it once it starts. \n\nIt's actually kind of like how the kids are when someone brings in cupcakes for a birthday. It's just complete chaos. Last week it was Leo's birthday, and his mom brought in these massive cupcakes with that really thick, stiff frosting. You know the kind that stains your teeth and hands blue for two days? It was a disaster. I had to send three kids to the nurse because they got frosting in their hair, and one kid somehow got it on his shoe and tracked it all over the reading rug. That rug was so expensive, too. I bought it myself at Target during their back-to-school sale. They always have the cutest stuff in that dollar spot section at the front, but you have to get there in July or all the good bins and borders are gone. I spent like forty dollars just on these little motivational posters with sloths on them. Sloths are huge this year for some reason. Last year it was llamas. I don't know who decides these trends, but I swear half my paycheck goes to keeping my classroom looking cute. \n\nBut, um, yeah. It's definitely a loss of control. I'm not choosing to keep eating. It just happens and I can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed, rather than another emotion?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's stress or being overwhelmed, either. Like I said before, I just haven't really noticed a specific feeling that starts it all. I wish I could give you a better answer, but it's just really hard to pinpoint. \n\nIt's kind of like trying to figure out why the copy machine at school jams. It just happens, and you never know what caused it. And our copy machine is ancient. I'm pretty sure it's older than some of the newer teachers. Last week, it jammed right before I needed to print out the math worksheets for our fractions unit. Fractions are always so hard for the kids to grasp. I tried using those little plastic pie pieces to show them, but half the pieces are missing from the math kits. I think the kids from last year might have taken them. Kids love taking little things like that. One time I found a kid with a pocket full of those counting bears. The red ones, specifically. I don't know why he only wanted the red ones. Maybe it was his favorite color? My favorite color is green, like a really deep emerald green. My mom had this emerald green velvet dress she used to wear to holiday parties when I was little, and I always thought she looked like a movie star in it. I wonder if she still has it. I should ask her. She's been wanting me to come over and help her clean out the attic anyway. \n\nBut, um... yeah, no. I really haven't noticed if it's stress or feeling overwhelmed that triggers the eating. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you're not paying attention to how full you're getting, or is it more that you're intentionally trying to eat as much as you can?\n\nPatient: Um, wait, I think we just talked about this, right? The control thing? Yeah, I'm definitely not trying to eat that much on purpose, and it's not about paying attention. Like I said before, it's just... once I start, it's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside. Just standing there going, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm so full it hurts. I just can't stop. \n\nWhich is exactly what I tell my kids when they're running in the halls—\"You have to stop your bodies!\" They just don't listen sometimes. Especially on rainy days when we have indoor recess. Indoor recess is a nightmare. They just play Heads Up Seven Up for twenty minutes, but someone always ends up crying because they think someone peeked at their shoes. Kids are so obsessed with shoes now. One of my fourth graders has these light-up sneakers that he charges with a USB cord. Can you imagine? Having to plug your shoes in at night? When I was their age, we just had those jelly sandals that gave you blisters if you wore them without socks. I had a pink pair that I wore to Disney World when I was seven, and my dad had to carry me through Epcot because my feet hurt so bad. Epcot is really boring when you're seven, anyway. It's just a bunch of fake countries and that big golf ball thing. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's definitely not intentional. It's just that loss of control, like I'm watching myself do it and I can't make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you're in a state where you're not thinking clearly about your actions?\n\nPatient: Um, I feel like we're kind of going in circles here? It's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just standing there watching from the outside, literally saying, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep going. I always buy those big boxes of Ticonderoga pencils—they're the only ones that don't break every five seconds in the sharpener. Like I said, I *am* thinking clearly when it happens, I just... I can't make myself stop eating. It's just completely out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating affect your daily life or cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, it definitely causes a lot of distress. It really messes with everything. Like, my evenings and my weekends, they just feel completely planned around trying to undo whatever happened with the food. So I've been getting really behind on things. I'm way slower with my grading, which is stressful because report cards are coming up. And I'm just less present with my friends, too. I'm constantly worried about making sure I still seem totally put-together at work, you know? \n\nWhich is exhausting, honestly. Being put-together at school is basically a full-time job in itself. You have to look like you know what you're doing at all times, even when the projector stops working in the middle of a lesson. Our projector is always breaking. The bulb went out last Tuesday, and the tech guy, Mr. Henderson, took three days to come fix it. He's always so slow. We go through whiteboard markers so fast. The kids love to push too hard on the tips and ruin them. Especially the blue ones. Why is it always the blue ones? Blue is such a calming color, though. I painted my bedroom a really soft, pale blue last summer. It took me three coats to get it right because the old paint underneath was this terrible mustard yellow. Who paints a bedroom mustard yellow? It looked like a giant hot dog stand. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. It affects my life a lot. I'm just always worried about hiding it and trying to fix it, and it makes it hard to keep up with work and my friends.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's pretty frequent. She knitted me a sweater once that had one sleeve longer than the other, but I wore it anyway because she was so proud of it. She passed away a few years ago, actually. We had to clean out her house, and you wouldn't believe how much yarn one person can hoard. It was like a craft store exploded in her guest bedroom. It sounds like a dying whale. I saw a documentary about whales once on Netflix. They have these incredibly complex songs that can travel for miles underwater. It's fascinating how they communicate. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. The guilt is really bad. Elm Street has all those huge, old oak trees that hang over the road, and in the fall, the leaves get so thick on the ground it's like driving on a carpet. The city is terrible at clearing them. Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. That's why I always keep a space heater under my desk at school. He's always complaining about something, usually the amount of glitter we use in art projects. Glitter really is the herpes of the craft world, it just never goes away. I found a piece of purple glitter on my cheek just yesterday, and we haven't done a glitter project since Valentine's Day. Valentine's Day is exhausting in an elementary school. I had a student last year, little Kevin, who used to try to eat the glue sticks. I had to watch him like a hawk during arts and crafts. He also used to chew on his pencils until the metal part holding the eraser was completely flattened. I worry about kids like that, you know? Like, what is going on at home that makes you want to eat glue? I brought it up to the school counselor, Mrs. Higgins, but she's always so busy. It's like I was telling you, once I start, the reasonable part of me—the teacher part—is just standing there watching, going, \"Joan, you need to stop.\" But my hands don't listen. They just keep opening wrappers and shoving food in my mouth until it actually hurts. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I'm usually so good at being in control! Like with my classroom. You have to be, right? If you lose control of a room full of eight-year-olds for even one second, they sense weakness. It's like they're little sharks. Cute sharks, but still. Last year, I had this student, Maya, who decided she was going to lead a mutiny during our spelling test. She just stood up on her chair and started singing \"Let It Go\" at the top of her lungs. And then three other kids joined in! I had to use my serious teacher voice—the one that comes from the diaphragm, you know? My choir director in college taught me how to project like that. He was this very intense man who always wore turtlenecks, even in May. He said it protected his vocal cords, but I think he just liked how they looked. He had this whole collection of them in different shades of gray and black. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... with the eating, that teacher voice just doesn't work on me. I just keep going until I'm so stuffed I can't even move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating like this in response to feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I don't really know? Like I was saying earlier about the stress, I just haven't noticed if it's specifically when I'm sad or anxious. It's so hard to pinpoint emotions sometimes. \n\nWhich is kind of ironic, because we actually spend so much time teaching the kids how to do exactly that. We started this new Social-Emotional Learning curriculum this year, and every morning we do a \"feelings check-in\" with this giant mood meter poster on the whiteboard. It has all these different color zones—like the blue zone is for sad or tired, and the red zone is for angry. I had to laminate all the little name magnets for it myself because the big laminator in the teachers' lounge has been jammed since October. Brenda, our front office secretary, put this huge, passive-aggressive neon pink sticky note on it that just says \"OUT OF ORDER: DO NOT EVEN TRY.\" She's terrifying, honestly. She's been at the school for like thirty years and basically runs the place. If you need fresh dry erase markers, you have to practically beg her for them. She keeps the good Expo ones locked in this metal cabinet behind her desk like they're gold bars. You only get the cheap off-brand ones that dry out in a day unless you're on her good side. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my feelings. Yeah, I just... I really haven't noticed a specific pattern with being upset or anxious before the eating starts. I'm not really sure what sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's *always*. Like, it's hard to say if it happens every single time. Sometimes I think I'm just too exhausted afterward to even feel guilty, you know? Just completely drained. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the exhaustion I get after parent-teacher conferences. Oh my gosh, those are just so draining. And they make us sit in the students' chairs for them! I don't know whose idea that was, but my knees are practically up to my chin for three hours straight. And the parents are always so intense. Like Mrs. Peterson—she came in last month demanding to know why her son isn't reading at a fifth-grade level yet. He's eight! I had to politely explain that he's doing perfectly fine where he is. She actually brought me these homemade snickerdoodles to try and, like, bribe me into giving him extra credit or something. They were so dry, honestly. You can always tell when someone uses shortening instead of real butter. My grandmother used to swear by shortening for her pie crusts, which did make them super flaky, but for cookies, you really need real butter. I usually buy that Irish butter in the gold foil? But it's gotten so expensive. I saw it at the store the other day and it was almost eight dollars. Eight dollars for a block of butter! It's just ridiculous how much groceries cost right now. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, if I always feel guilty. Yeah, I'm just not sure if it's every single time. Sometimes I'm just too tired to feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotion, such as anxiety or sadness?\n\nPatient: Um, wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like, about the emotions? I think I just... yeah, I really haven't noticed if it's sadness or anxiety or whatever. It's just hard to tell. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because, like I said, we're doing all this feelings stuff at school. The kids actually love it, though. Well, most of them. Maya—the one who sang \"Let It Go\"—she decided her feeling color is \"sparkle,\" which isn't even on the chart. But what are you going to do? You have to pick your battles with eight-year-olds. If you fight them on everything, you just end up exhausted by recess. And recess duty is the worst anyway. You just stand out there in the freezing cold while they run around like maniacs. Last week, it was so windy my scarf blew right off my neck and got stuck in the chain-link fence. It was my favorite scarf, too—this really soft cashmere one my sister got me for Christmas. She always gets the best gifts. She works in marketing, so she always knows what's trendy before anyone else does. She actually tried to get me to move to the city with her after grad school, but I just couldn't imagine teaching in a huge district like that. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I just really haven't noticed a specific emotion that triggers it. I wish I had a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control of your eating during these episodes, or is it more that you're making choices to continue eating despite feeling full?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it's definitely not a choice. It's like... once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Just standing there saying, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. I just can't stop it once it starts. \n\nIt's actually kind of like how the kids are when someone brings in cupcakes for a birthday. It's just complete chaos. Last week it was Leo's birthday, and his mom brought in these massive cupcakes with that really thick, stiff frosting. You know the kind that stains your teeth and hands blue for two days? It was a disaster. I had to send three kids to the nurse because they got frosting in their hair, and one kid somehow got it on his shoe and tracked it all over the reading rug. That rug was so expensive, too. I bought it myself at Target during their back-to-school sale. They always have the cutest stuff in that dollar spot section at the front, but you have to get there in July or all the good bins and borders are gone. I spent like forty dollars just on these little motivational posters with sloths on them. Sloths are huge this year for some reason. Last year it was llamas. I don't know who decides these trends, but I swear half my paycheck goes to keeping my classroom looking cute. \n\nBut, um, yeah. It's definitely a loss of control. I'm not choosing to keep eating. It just happens and I can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed, rather than another emotion?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know if it's stress or being overwhelmed, either. Like I said before, I just haven't really noticed a specific feeling that starts it all. I wish I could give you a better answer, but it's just really hard to pinpoint. \n\nIt's kind of like trying to figure out why the copy machine at school jams. It just happens, and you never know what caused it. And our copy machine is ancient. I'm pretty sure it's older than some of the newer teachers. Last week, it jammed right before I needed to print out the math worksheets for our fractions unit. Fractions are always so hard for the kids to grasp. I tried using those little plastic pie pieces to show them, but half the pieces are missing from the math kits. I think the kids from last year might have taken them. Kids love taking little things like that. One time I found a kid with a pocket full of those counting bears. The red ones, specifically. I don't know why he only wanted the red ones. Maybe it was his favorite color? My favorite color is green, like a really deep emerald green. My mom had this emerald green velvet dress she used to wear to holiday parties when I was little, and I always thought she looked like a movie star in it. I wonder if she still has it. I should ask her. She's been wanting me to come over and help her clean out the attic anyway. \n\nBut, um... yeah, no. I really haven't noticed if it's stress or feeling overwhelmed that triggers the eating. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you're not paying attention to how full you're getting, or is it more that you're intentionally trying to eat as much as you can?\n\nPatient: Um, wait, I think we just talked about this, right? The control thing? Yeah, I'm definitely not trying to eat that much on purpose, and it's not about paying attention. Like I said before, it's just... once I start, it's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside. Just standing there going, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm so full it hurts. I just can't stop. \n\nWhich is exactly what I tell my kids when they're running in the halls—\"You have to stop your bodies!\" They just don't listen sometimes. Especially on rainy days when we have indoor recess. Indoor recess is a nightmare. They just play Heads Up Seven Up for twenty minutes, but someone always ends up crying because they think someone peeked at their shoes. Kids are so obsessed with shoes now. One of my fourth graders has these light-up sneakers that he charges with a USB cord. Can you imagine? Having to plug your shoes in at night? When I was their age, we just had those jelly sandals that gave you blisters if you wore them without socks. I had a pink pair that I wore to Disney World when I was seven, and my dad had to carry me through Epcot because my feet hurt so bad. Epcot is really boring when you're seven, anyway. It's just a bunch of fake countries and that big golf ball thing. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it's definitely not intentional. It's just that loss of control, like I'm watching myself do it and I can't make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you're in a state where you're not thinking clearly about your actions?\n\nPatient: Um, I feel like we're kind of going in circles here? I know I already said this a couple of times, but it's not really about thinking clearly or not thinking clearly. It's just... I *am* thinking, but I can't do anything about it. It's like that reasonable teacher part of me is just standing there watching from the outside, literally saying, \"Joan, stop,\" but my hands just keep going. They just keep opening wrappers and putting food in my mouth until I'm so full it's actually painful. I just can't stop it.\n\nWhich is so frustrating, because normally I'm the one in charge, you know? Like during the fire drills at school. I have to be the one directing everyone, making sure all twenty-two kids are lined up and quiet. We had a fire drill last month right in the middle of our spelling test, which was a total disaster because half the kids left their pencils on their desks and then couldn't find them when we came back inside. I always buy those big boxes of Ticonderoga pencils—they're the only ones that don't break every five seconds in the sharpener. The cheap ones always snap, and then you have a line of kids waiting at the sharpener, which is right next to the guinea pig cage. We have a class guinea pig named Barnaby. He squeaks every time someone sharpens a pencil because he thinks the noise means he's getting a carrot. Guinea pigs are so funny like that. My sister had one when we were growing up, but it escaped and lived under our sofa for three days before my dad finally caught it with a fishing net. \n\nBut, um, yeah. Like I said, I *am* thinking clearly when it happens, I just... I can't make myself stop eating. It's just completely out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating affect your daily life or cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, it definitely causes a lot of distress. It really messes with everything. Like, my evenings and my weekends, they just feel completely planned around trying to undo whatever happened with the food. So I've been getting really behind on things. I'm way slower with my grading, which is stressful because report cards are coming up. And I'm just less present with my friends, too. I'm constantly worried about making sure I still seem totally put-together at work, you know? \n\nWhich is exhausting, honestly. Being put-together at school is basically a full-time job in itself. You have to look like you know what you're doing at all times, even when the projector stops working in the middle of a lesson. Our projector is always breaking. The bulb went out last Tuesday, and the tech guy, Mr. Henderson, took three days to come fix it. He's always so slow. I think he spends most of his time in his office playing solitaire on his computer. I saw him doing it once when I went down there to ask for more whiteboard markers. We go through whiteboard markers so fast. The kids love to push too hard on the tips and ruin them. Especially the blue ones. Why is it always the blue ones? Blue is such a calming color, though. I painted my bedroom a really soft, pale blue last summer. It took me three coats to get it right because the old paint underneath was this terrible mustard yellow. Who paints a bedroom mustard yellow? It looked like a giant hot dog stand. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. It affects my life a lot. I'm just always worried about hiding it and trying to fix it, and it makes it hard to keep up with work and my friends.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's pretty frequent. For the past few months, there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I eat way, way more than I ever meant to. And then, of course, the next day I try to make up for it by just not eating at school, or pushing myself through a really long walk or extra yoga when I get home. \n\nYoga is supposed to be so relaxing, right? But I go to this studio downtown, and the instructor, she always plays this really aggressive, thumping electronic music during the vinyasa flow. It's so weird. You're trying to hold a warrior pose and breathe, and it sounds like you're at a nightclub in Berlin. I went to Berlin once during college for a study abroad trip. We took a train there from Paris, and I lost my favorite scarf on that train. It was this beautiful chunky knit infinity scarf that my grandmother made me. She was always knitting things. She knitted me a sweater once that had one sleeve longer than the other, but I wore it anyway because she was so proud of it. She passed away a few years ago, actually. We had to clean out her house, and you wouldn't believe how much yarn one person can hoard. It was like a craft store exploded in her guest bedroom. We ended up donating most of it to a local nursing home. I hope they made some nice blankets or something out of it. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so it's at least once a week, usually, that I have one of those nights, and then I have to do the fasting or the extra exercise the next day to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah. I mean, it's terrible. I feel so guilty, and I'm just so hard on myself afterwards. Because it all comes back to how I look, you know? Like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't matter how well my students are doing or how prepared my lesson plans are. If my clothes are tight, I just feel like a total failure. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the kids do not care what I look like. They just care if I brought stickers. I have this huge binder of scratch-and-sniff stickers. The grape ones are the most popular, obviously. But I bought this one pack online that was supposed to smell like pizza, and it was the most disgusting thing I've ever smelled in my life. It smelled like old garlic and sweaty gym socks. I gave one to a kid, Tommy, and he stuck it on his forehead and then cried because the smell wouldn't wash off. His mom had to email me about it. Parents send the wildest emails sometimes. I got one last week from a mom asking if I could make sure her daughter only drinks room-temperature water from the fountain because cold water \"shocks her aura.\" Like, how am I supposed to control the temperature of the school water fountain? The fountain in our hallway barely even works anyway. You have to press the button at the exact right angle or it just makes this loud groaning noise and spits out a tiny trickle. It sounds like a dying whale. I saw a documentary about whales once on Netflix. They have these incredibly complex songs that can travel for miles underwater. It's fascinating how they communicate. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. The guilt is really bad. I just judge myself so harshly if my body changes even a little bit, and it completely ruins my confidence, even when I know I'm doing a good job at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating because you're hungry or because you just can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not because I'm hungry. It's... it's like a total loss of control. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. It's like I'm on autopilot. \n\nAutopilot is such a weird concept, isn't it? Like, when you're driving home from work and you suddenly pull into your driveway and realize you don't remember the last ten minutes of the drive. That happens to me all the time on Elm Street. Elm Street has all those huge, old oak trees that hang over the road, and in the fall, the leaves get so thick on the ground it's like driving on a carpet. The city is terrible at clearing them. Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. That's why I always keep a space heater under my desk at school. The custodian, Mr. Davies, told me it's a fire hazard, but I just hide it behind my filing cabinet when he comes in to empty the trash. He's always complaining about something, usually the amount of glitter we use in art projects. Glitter really is the herpes of the craft world, it just never goes away. I found a piece of purple glitter on my cheek just yesterday, and we haven't done a glitter project since Valentine's Day. Valentine's Day is exhausting in an elementary school. The amount of sugar those kids consume...\n\nBut, um, yeah, anyway. I'm definitely not hungry when it happens. I just can't stop my hands from grabbing the food once I've started, even when my brain is telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're deliberately choosing to continue eating during these episodes, or does it feel like you're unable to stop?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, like I was saying, it's... it's really not a choice. It feels totally out of my hands. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Like I'm standing there saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. \n\nIt's actually a lot like how I feel when I'm trying to organize the supply closet at work. You go in there just needing one pack of dry erase markers, and suddenly you're knee-deep in construction paper and trying to sort out three years' worth of dried-up glue sticks. I don't know why schools buy those purple glue sticks anyway. They say they dry clear, but they always leave this weird crusty residue on the paper. I had a student last year, little Kevin, who used to try to eat the glue sticks. I had to watch him like a hawk during arts and crafts. He also used to chew on his pencils until the metal part holding the eraser was completely flattened. I worry about kids like that, you know? Like, what is going on at home that makes you want to eat glue? I brought it up to the school counselor, Mrs. Higgins, but she's always so busy. She has this tiny office that smells strongly of lavender, which I think is supposed to be calming, but it just gives me a headache. I'm really sensitive to smells. I can't even walk down the detergent aisle at the grocery store without getting a migraine. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so I'm definitely not choosing to keep eating. It just... it just happens, and I can't make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure if there's a specific emotion, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that. It just kind of happens. I mean, I guess I'm always a little stressed, but who isn't? Teaching is stressful. \n\nEspecially right now, because we have state testing coming up, and the administration is breathing down our necks about the math scores. They brought in this new curriculum halfway through the year, which is just brilliant, right? Let's change how we do math in February. It's supposed to be more \"inquiry-based,\" but the kids are just confused, and honestly, so am I. I spent three hours on Sunday just trying to figure out how to explain a fraction model that looks like a weird pizza. Speaking of pizza, they changed the cafeteria vendor this year, and the new pizza is basically cardboard with ketchup on it. The kids actually started a petition to bring back the old pizza. A third grader wrote it! It was so cute, spelled entirely phonetically, but the principal was not amused. He's this very stern guy, Mr. Henderson, who always wears these incredibly loud ties. Like, neon geometric patterns. It's very distracting when he's observing your class. I always wonder where he even buys them. Does he have a special catalog for ugly ties? \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. I don't really know if it's tied to being sad or anxious or anything. I just haven't really paid attention to that part of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
