And then, of course, the next day I try to make up for it by just not eating at school, or pushing myself through a really long walk or extra yoga when I get home. \n\nYoga is supposed to be so relaxing, right? But I go to this studio downtown, and the instructor, she always plays this really aggressive, thumping electronic music during the vinyasa flow. It's so weird. You're trying to hold a warrior pose and breathe, and it sounds like you're at a nightclub in Berlin. I went to Berlin once during college for a study abroad trip. We took a train there from Paris, and I lost my favorite scarf on that train. It was this beautiful chunky knit infinity scarf that my grandmother made me. She was always knitting things. She knitted me a sweater once that had one sleeve longer than the other, but I wore it anyway because she was so proud of it. She passed away a few years ago, actually. We had to clean out her house, and you wouldn't believe how much yarn one person can hoard. It was like a craft store exploded in her guest bedroom. We ended up donating most of it to a local nursing home. I hope they made some nice blankets or something out of it. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so it's at least once a week, usually, that I have one of those nights, and then I have to do the fasting or the extra exercise the next day to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah. I mean, it's terrible. I feel so guilty, and I'm just so hard on myself afterwards. Because it all comes back to how I look, you know? Like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't matter how well my students are doing or how prepared my lesson plans are. If my clothes are tight, I just feel like a total failure. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the kids do not care what I look like. They just care if I brought stickers. Elm Street has all those huge, old oak trees that hang over the road, and in the fall, the leaves get so thick on the ground it's like driving on a carpet. The city is terrible at clearing them. Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. That's why I always keep a space heater under my desk at school. The custodian, Mr. Davies, told me it's a fire hazard, but I just hide it behind my filing cabinet when he comes in to empty the trash. He's always complaining about something, usually the amount of glitter we use in art projects. Glitter really is the herpes of the craft world, it just never goes away. I found a piece of purple glitter on my cheek just yesterday, and we haven't done a glitter project since Valentine's Day. Valentine's Day is exhausting in an elementary school. I'm constantly worried about making sure I still seem totally put-together at work, you know? \n\nWhich is exhausting, honestly. Being put-together at school is basically a full-time job in itself. You have to look like you know what you're doing at all times, even when the projector stops working in the middle of a lesson. Our projector is always breaking. The bulb went out last Tuesday, and the tech guy, Mr. Henderson, took three days to come fix it. He's always so slow. I think he spends most of his time in his office playing solitaire on his computer. I saw him doing it once when I went down there to ask for more whiteboard markers. We go through whiteboard markers so fast. The kids love to push too hard on the tips and ruin them. Especially the blue ones. Why is it always the blue ones? Blue is such a calming color, though. I painted my bedroom a really soft, pale blue last summer. It took me three coats to get it right because the old paint underneath was this terrible mustard yellow. Who paints a bedroom mustard yellow? It looked like a giant hot dog stand. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. It affects my life a lot. I'm just always worried about hiding it and trying to fix it, and it makes it hard to keep up with work and my friends.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um, well, it's pretty frequent. For the past few months, there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I eat way, way more than I ever meant to. And then, of course, the next day I try to make up for it by just not eating at school, or pushing myself through a really long walk or extra yoga when I get home. \n\nYoga is supposed to be so relaxing, right? But I go to this studio downtown, and the instructor, she always plays this really aggressive, thumping electronic music during the vinyasa flow. It's so weird. You're trying to hold a warrior pose and breathe, and it sounds like you're at a nightclub in Berlin. I went to Berlin once during college for a study abroad trip. We took a train there from Paris, and I lost my favorite scarf on that train. It was this beautiful chunky knit infinity scarf that my grandmother made me. She was always knitting things. She knitted me a sweater once that had one sleeve longer than the other, but I wore it anyway because she was so proud of it. She passed away a few years ago, actually. We had to clean out her house, and you wouldn't believe how much yarn one person can hoard. It was like a craft store exploded in her guest bedroom. We ended up donating most of it to a local nursing home. I hope they made some nice blankets or something out of it. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so it's at least once a week, usually, that I have one of those nights, and then I have to do the fasting or the extra exercise the next day to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel guilty or criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah. I mean, it's terrible. I feel so guilty, and I'm just so hard on myself afterwards. Because it all comes back to how I look, you know? Like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't matter how well my students are doing or how prepared my lesson plans are. If my clothes are tight, I just feel like a total failure. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the kids do not care what I look like. They just care if I brought stickers. I have this huge binder of scratch-and-sniff stickers. The grape ones are the most popular, obviously. But I bought this one pack online that was supposed to smell like pizza, and it was the most disgusting thing I've ever smelled in my life. It smelled like old garlic and sweaty gym socks. I gave one to a kid, Tommy, and he stuck it on his forehead and then cried because the smell wouldn't wash off. His mom had to email me about it. Parents send the wildest emails sometimes. I got one last week from a mom asking if I could make sure her daughter only drinks room-temperature water from the fountain because cold water \"shocks her aura.\" Like, how am I supposed to control the temperature of the school water fountain? The fountain in our hallway barely even works anyway. You have to press the button at the exact right angle or it just makes this loud groaning noise and spits out a tiny trickle. It sounds like a dying whale. I saw a documentary about whales once on Netflix. They have these incredibly complex songs that can travel for miles underwater. It's fascinating how they communicate. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. The guilt is really bad. I just judge myself so harshly if my body changes even a little bit, and it completely ruins my confidence, even when I know I'm doing a good job at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating because you're hungry or because you just can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not because I'm hungry. It's... it's like a total loss of control. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. It's like I'm on autopilot. \n\nAutopilot is such a weird concept, isn't it? Like, when you're driving home from work and you suddenly pull into your driveway and realize you don't remember the last ten minutes of the drive. That happens to me all the time on Elm Street. Elm Street has all those huge, old oak trees that hang over the road, and in the fall, the leaves get so thick on the ground it's like driving on a carpet. The city is terrible at clearing them. Last year, a city street sweeper actually got stuck in a giant pile of wet leaves right in front of my house. The driver had to get out and try to dig it out with a shovel. He looked so miserable. I brought him out a cup of coffee in a travel mug, but he wouldn't take it. He just grunted at me. People can be so rude sometimes, especially when they're cold. I hate being cold. That's why I always keep a space heater under my desk at school. The custodian, Mr. Davies, told me it's a fire hazard, but I just hide it behind my filing cabinet when he comes in to empty the trash. He's always complaining about something, usually the amount of glitter we use in art projects. Glitter really is the herpes of the craft world, it just never goes away. I found a piece of purple glitter on my cheek just yesterday, and we haven't done a glitter project since Valentine's Day. Valentine's Day is exhausting in an elementary school. The amount of sugar those kids consume...\n\nBut, um, yeah, anyway. I'm definitely not hungry when it happens. I just can't stop my hands from grabbing the food once I've started, even when my brain is telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're deliberately choosing to continue eating during these episodes, or does it feel like you're unable to stop?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, like I was saying, it's... it's really not a choice. It feels totally out of my hands. Once I start, it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, you know? Like I'm standing there saying, 'Joan, stop,' but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I'm painfully full. \n\nIt's actually a lot like how I feel when I'm trying to organize the supply closet at work. You go in there just needing one pack of dry erase markers, and suddenly you're knee-deep in construction paper and trying to sort out three years' worth of dried-up glue sticks. I don't know why schools buy those purple glue sticks anyway. They say they dry clear, but they always leave this weird crusty residue on the paper. I had a student last year, little Kevin, who used to try to eat the glue sticks. I had to watch him like a hawk during arts and crafts. He also used to chew on his pencils until the metal part holding the eraser was completely flattened. I worry about kids like that, you know? Like, what is going on at home that makes you want to eat glue? I brought it up to the school counselor, Mrs. Higgins, but she's always so busy. She has this tiny office that smells strongly of lavender, which I think is supposed to be calming, but it just gives me a headache. I'm really sensitive to smells. I can't even walk down the detergent aisle at the grocery store without getting a migraine. \n\nBut, um, yeah, so I'm definitely not choosing to keep eating. It just... it just happens, and I can't make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as feeling anxious, sad, or upset?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure if there's a specific emotion, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a pattern like that. It just kind of happens. I mean, I guess I'm always a little stressed, but who isn't? Teaching is stressful. \n\nEspecially right now, because we have state testing coming up, and the administration is breathing down our necks about the math scores. They brought in this new curriculum halfway through the year, which is just brilliant, right? Let's change how we do math in February. It's supposed to be more \"inquiry-based,\" but the kids are just confused, and honestly, so am I. I spent three hours on Sunday just trying to figure out how to explain a fraction model that looks like a weird pizza. Speaking of pizza, they changed the cafeteria vendor this year, and the new pizza is basically cardboard with ketchup on it. The kids actually started a petition to bring back the old pizza. A third grader wrote it! It was so cute, spelled entirely phonetically, but the principal was not amused. He's this very stern guy, Mr. Henderson, who always wears these incredibly loud ties. Like, neon geometric patterns. It's very distracting when he's observing your class. I always wonder where he even buys them. Does he have a special catalog for ugly ties? \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. I don't really know if it's tied to being sad or anxious or anything. I just haven't really paid attention to that part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state?\n\nPatient: Um, I think I just answered that, didn't I? I mean, I really don't know if there's a specific emotion that sets it off. I haven't noticed if I'm sad or anxious right before. It's just... I don't know, it just happens. \n\nI was actually just telling my friend Sarah about this—well, not about the eating, but about how hard it is to track my moods. She's really into journaling. She bought me this beautiful leather-bound notebook for my birthday last month. It has these thick, creamy pages that are almost too nice to write on. I tried using my favorite gel pen on it, but it smeared everywhere. I hate when that happens. You get a good thought going and then—smudge. It ruins the whole page. I had to rip it out, which made this awful tearing sound, and then the binding felt slightly loose. It's like when you pull a loose thread on a sweater and suddenly the whole sleeve is unraveling. That happened to a cardigan of mine last winter. It was my favorite one, this deep mustard yellow color. I tried to fix it myself, but I'm terrible at sewing. My grandmother tried to teach me when I was little, but I could never get the hang of threading the needle. She had this old Singer sewing machine that weighed a ton and sounded like a lawnmower...\n\nBut, yeah, sorry. Like I said, I don't really know if my mood has anything to do with the eating. I haven't noticed a connection."
  }
